2026-08-20 01:17:56 GPU 0 free, convergence check first

  Does a bigger batch change the answer? Checked before the run, not after.

  Per call, at batch 1, 10, 20 and 50, for cuBLAS and for gb300_accelerated:
    rot/N      the batch on N DISTINCT operand copies -- what the code does
    plain/N    the same batch on ONE operand set -- what a naive batch would report
    floor/N    an empty kernel batched the same way
    rot-floor  per call with that floor taken off: the number that should be flat in N
    drift      rot-floor against its own value at N=10, where the batch is in steady state

  Read `drift`: from N=10 to N=50, a five-fold change in the batch, the floor-corrected
  per-call time moves by at most 12% and usually 2-5%. That is convergence, and the shapes
  that move most are the ones pick_batch keeps at a small batch anyway.

  Read `rot` against `plain`: they part company exactly where the operands fit in this
  card's 129 MiB L2 and the kernel is memory-bound -- moe_up 128x8192x4096 at N=50 reads
  14.2 us on its own operands and 9.2 us sharing one set, 55% apart, because in the shared
  batch only call 1 is cold. On attn 256x8192x8192 (136 MiB, too big to cache) and on
  lm_head (1.8 GiB) the two agree to within 1%, which is what says the difference is L2 and
  not something else. A per-call flush would fix it the other way and cost a ~16 us memset
  against a 2 us kernel -- a bigger floor than the 6.9 us one being removed.

  device NVIDIA GB300   L2 129 MiB

gemm.perf.static -- 698 (model, layer, dtype) cases over 590 distinct (M, N, K, dtype) shapes, 5345 arm records
  planned: 698 cases over 590 shapes (391 fp16, 199 fp8); 0 fp8 candidates dropped for K % 16
  dtype rule: fp16 on every case, fp8 additionally on moe_up, moe_down, mlp_up, mlp_down, attn
  timing column: device_flush_ms

  The arms, and what a ratio here is
    cublas                 cuBLAS on the algorithm its own heuristic returns. THE BIT REFERENCE, and the baseline
    torch_auto             torch.compile as its autotuner picks it, extern included
    torch_triton           torch.compile with the extern excluded, so Inductor's own Triton template
    bitequiv_autotuning    bit-equivalent: the kernel BEFORE the sm_103 rewrites, configuration chosen by a search
    gb300_accelerated      bit-equivalent: the GB300-specific rewrites and the shipped fitted rule
    Every arm is reported against cuBLAS and against nothing else, as cuBLAS time over
    that arm's time, SO ABOVE 1 MEANS FASTER THAN CUBLAS. Ratios between two arms are not
    printed: they hide the levels, and where two arms differ in more than one way they
    invite a causal reading this measurement cannot support. Four numbers on one baseline
    let a reader form whatever comparison they want.
    bitequiv_autotuning and gb300_accelerated are BOTH byte-identical to cuBLAS and differ
    in how they got there: a search over a configuration space, against rewrites written
    for this architecture plus a fitted rule. They differ in two ways at once -- the
    kernel and the way its configuration is chosen -- so a comparison covers both.
    gb300_accelerated against a torch arm is NOT the price of bit-exactness. They are
    different kernels: ours has TMA descriptors, a persistent grid and warp specialization
    and Inductor's mm_template has none of them. It is a product comparison.

  per case. Times are microseconds per call; `x cuBLAS` is cuBLAS over that arm, above 1
  faster than cuBLAS. No G, B or T in flags means every draw of that arm was byte-identical.

  Check `byte-identical` equals the total first: where an arm was not byte-identical to
  cuBLAS its time is not a measurement of anything. It counts input draws, ten per case.
  flags  F near the launch floor (under 3x floor, every ratio compressed toward 1)
         ! an arm the CUDA graph would not capture   d declined   e error
         G gb300_accelerated, B bitequiv_autotuning, T torch_auto was not byte-identical
           to cuBLAS on every draw

  batch is `batch_n`: how many calls went into one CUDA graph, each on its own operand
  copy, with the replay divided by that. A one-call replay costs about 6.9 us on this box
  before any work of ours runs, which is 2% of an lm_head and 77% of a 2 us expert GEMM,
  and every arm pays the same 6.9, so a true 2x on a small shape reads as 1.22x. The batch
  divides that fixed cost; a kernel already far above the floor gets batch 1, unchanged.
  `-` is a row taken before the batch existed and it is NOT comparable to a batched row on
  a small shape. floor is floor_ms at that row's own batch, so F means the same thing at
  every batch. It does not fall to zero: graph nodes run one after another and an empty
  kernel still takes about 1 us, which a real caller pays too. A GEMM the size of a LoRA
  merge stays flagged after batching, and it should -- it is not distinguishable from a
  launch.
